This is the opposite case: `technicalFouls` is the **twelfth** per-player accumulator on `PlayerGameState`, and **the other eleven are all already persisted and exposed** (points, off/def rebounds, assists, steals, blocks, turnovers, fouls, FGA/FGM, 3PA/3PM, FTA/FTM). Leaving exactly one out makes the box score **inconsistent rather than lean** — a stats page rendering fouls-but-not-technicals is an obvious gap, and the user's judgement ("there will be a consumer") is a product call about a set that is already complete but for this member. **#014/#017 guards against inventing a field with no analogue; it does not require the last member of an existing, uniformly-surfaced set to wait.**

**Decision C — it is a DENORMALIZED convenience on the end-of-game snapshot, and the events stay the source of truth (#020).** The information was **already queryable before this change**: every technical emits a `FOUL` event carrying `outcome = 'TECHNICAL_FOUL'` and the committer's `primary_player_id`, so a per-player count is one `WHERE` clause. The column adds no new fact — it adds the same cheap read the other eleven get. Recorded explicitly so a later reader does not mistake the column for the authority: **if the two ever disagree, the events win** (#020), exactly as for `fouls`. The new integration test asserts they agree.

**Decision D — the counter split (#032 E) is preserved at every layer, and the schema says so.** `fouls` and `technical_fouls` are two columns, never one. The changeset comment, the entity javadoc and the OpenAPI `description` on **both** properties all state that `fouls` means personal fouls only and that a technical does not feed the six-foul disqualification. This is deliberate redundancy: #032 E's split is invisible at the schema level (two nullable SMALLINTs look interchangeable), and the one thing that would silently undo §3.14a's calibration is a future reader summing them.

**Rationale**: *(A)* A field exposed in one surface and not another is a latent inconsistency, and the cost of the extra two lines is trivial against the cost of a consumer discovering the gap later. *(B)* The set-completeness argument is materially different from "we might want this someday" — the standard #014/#017 rejects — and stating which argument is being made keeps the rule intact for the next case. *(C)* The pre-existing event-log queryability is what makes this genuinely additive and low-risk: nothing depends on the column, so it cannot be wrong, only absent. *(D)* The split is the load-bearing part of #032 E and the schema is where it is least obvious.
**Trade-off**: *(A)* An OpenAPI change means a regenerated client model, so it is not quite as free as a bare column add; and the field will be `null` on every box score written before this changeset ran (nullable, matching every other stat column — no backfill was done, and none is meaningful since those games' events still carry the truth). *(B)* Unparking a follow-up one phase after parking it is a small process inconsistency; recorded rather than quietly done. *(C)* Two representations of one fact is exactly the duplicate-source shape #013/#015 warn about — accepted here because the box score is uniformly a derived snapshot, so it is the established pattern rather than a new exception. *(D)* None.
**Alternatives considered**: **Persist the column but leave it off the OpenAPI model** (`committing_team_id`'s parked state, #028 D) — rejected (user call): it is the half-surfaced state the parity argument is against. **Leave it fully parked until a Phase-4 consumer exists** (#032's follow-up as written) — rejected: the set-completeness argument (B) is stronger than the general speculative-field rule it was invoking. **Fold technicals into `fouls` and let clients subtract** — rejected outright: it is precisely what #032 E refused, and would move two §3.13-calibrated numbers. **Backfill existing rows** — rejected: the games' event logs already carry the fact (#020), and a backfill would assert a value for rows written before the counter existed.
**Status of #033 decisions**: A–D all resolved and **BUILT in the same session** (this is a small additive change, not a phase — it does not follow the three-session rhythm). Net schema change: **one nullable column**, `gametime.box_score.technical_fouls SMALLINT`, appended to `release.1.0.4.game.sql` as **changeset `1.04.4`** (before the `-triggers` changeset, which stays last). Net OpenAPI change: **one property** on `BoxScore`, plus a clarifying `description` on the existing `fouls`. Touched: `release.1.0.4.game.sql`, `BoxScoreEntity`, `gametime.yaml`, `EntityMapper.entityToBoxScore`, `GameSimulator`'s box-score write. **No `sim` engine behavior changed** — no constant, no roll, no predicate, and none of §3.14a's landed numbers move. Verified: 513 tests green, `All coverage checks have been met.`, and a new integration test asserts the persisted column reconciles exactly with the `TECHNICAL_FOUL` event count while `fouls` continues to reconcile with personal-foul events only.

**#033 follow-up (carry forward)**:
- **`committing_team_id` is still NOT on the OpenAPI `GameEvent`** (#028/#029 follow-up). This entry's parity argument is about the **box score's stat set**, and does not automatically extend to it — a `GameEvent` column has no equivalent "eleven siblings already exposed" case. Decide it on its own merits when a consumer appears.
- **Ejections are still not represented anywhere** — not an event, not a column. ~~§3.14b's flagrant-2 will force the stored-state question~~ — **it did not** (#034 F): the predicate stayed derived there too, so there is still no stored ejection state anywhere in the model. **But §3.14b did strengthen the case for an `EJECTION` event**, from a different direction than expected: an ejection is now derivable from **two** causes (a player's `TECHNICAL_FOUL` count against `TECHNICAL_EJECTION_LIMIT`, **or** any single `FLAGRANT_FOUL_2`), so "was this player ejected, and why" became a **two-query** question rather than one. That plurality — not stored state — is the argument if a consumer ever wants it. Also newly relevant: at **0.027/team/game** across both causes the event is no longer vanishingly rare (§3.14a alone measured 0.014).
- **Rows written before changeset `1.04.4` have `technical_fouls = null`**, not 0. Any consumer must null-guard (as `EntityMapper` and the harness already do for every stat column).

### 034 — Flagrant fouls (§3.14b): a severity roll layered on ALL THREE existing foul sites, the first free-throw path in the model that RETURNS the ball — expressed as the same `continue` the offensive rebound already uses, under the same cap — and the predicted #023 F stored-state exception REFUSED A SECOND TIME, because a flagrant-2 that ejects immediately is a monotonic counter like every other disqualification
**Date**: 2026-08  
**Scope**: §3.14b is the second half of the §3.14 split (#032 A) and the **last new mechanic in Phase 3** — §3.15 (profiles) and §3.16 (recalibration) add none. §3.14a took the self-contained half; **this pass owns the two things that were deliberately isolated into it**: the possession-retention fork (a foul that awards free throws **and** returns the ball, breaking #030 B's invariant that every FT path ends the possession — no existing code does this) and the flagrant-2 severity grade. This is **engine work in the `sim` package**: **no schema change, no OpenAPI change, no new `PlayType`** (a flagrant is a *kind of foul* — the #025 F / #026 E / #028 D / #032 reuse discipline), **no new box-score column** (Decision H, and #033's parity argument explicitly does **not** extend here), **no new coach attribute** (#018's five stand), and **no new player attribute or skill**. Two new `outcome` strings, one new `FreeThrowSource`, one new counter, two new `SimConfig` constants. NOT YET BUILT — this entry is the resolved design; the execute-ready plan is todo.md's §3.14b execution plan.

**Decision A — the flagrant is an ADDITIONAL severity roll on a foul that already happened, hung off ALL THREE existing foul sites, and it changes no existing foul rate (user call, carried in from todo.md).** The existing foul model is untouched: `FoulResolver.isFoul` (the stopped shot), `isAndOne` (the foul the shot survived) and `resolveReboundFoul` (the loose-ball foul) fire exactly as often as they do today, and each then asks one further question — *was that foul a flagrant?* **This is the §3.7-block / §3.10-rebound-foul / §3.11-and-1 "carved off the top" shape for the fourth time**, with one deliberate inversion: the previous three carve a slice **out of** an outcome, whereas this one layers **on top of** an outcome that is already fully resolved. Nothing is re-partitioned, so **no existing rate moves by construction** — the same property that made §3.9 free. **All three sites, not a subset** (user call: "a flagrant could occur on any foul"), and the coverage is what makes the four possession cases fall out rather than being enumerated by hand: the stopped-shot and and-1 sites are **always defensive-on-a-shooter**, so they produce the retain cases; the rebounding site is the **only** one that can be offensive (#028 A2's over-the-back, ~22% of rebounding fouls as measured), so it is the only source of the possession-flipping case. **One shared roll and one shared constant across the three sites**, not three tunable rates: the sites differ in what the foul *was*, not in how likely a foul is to be excessive, and three constants at ~33 events per harness run could never be told apart (Decision G's resolvability arithmetic).

**Decision B — POSSESSION RETENTION IS THE `continue` THE OFFENSIVE REBOUND ALREADY USES, and it respects `MAX_OFFENSIVE_REBOUNDS_PER_POSSESSION` (user framing, adopted; the structural crux of §3.14b).** The user's framing, adopted directly: *if the offense keeps possession we could just start at the beginning of the possession flow with the same team possessing the ball.* **That is exactly what `resolvePossession`'s `while (true)` loop already is** — a "same team, run the flow again" machine that §3.7 block recovery, §3.8 OOB-offense and §3.10's rebounding foul all re-enter with `offensiveRebounds++; continue;`. **So the invariant #030 B stated — every FT path ends the possession — is broken not by building a new mechanism but by letting an FT path reach a `continue` that has existed since §3.3.** A flagrant retention re-runs `pickShooter` / `pickShotType` / `pickDefender` against the same offense, which is the correct model: after the free throws the offense inbounds and runs a new action.

**Retention respects the cap, and the reasoning is that the cap is a runaway-possession guard rather than a rebounding rule.** `MAX_OFFENSIVE_REBOUNDS_PER_POSSESSION` (3) is the only bound on the loop; every retention path in the engine counts against it, and its name is historical rather than semantic (§3.7's block recovery and §3.8's OOB-offense are not rebounds either, and both count). **Exempting the flagrant would make it the one path that can extend a possession without bound** — for a mechanic ~200× rarer than an offensive rebound, buying nothing measurable. Concretely: at ~0.16 flagrants per team-game against ~19 fouls, the case where a flagrant retention is refused *because the cap was already full* arises on the order of **once every several simulated games**, so the cap's presence is nearly unobservable and its absence would be a latent unbounded loop. **The cap-reached case degrades exactly as §3.10's does** — the free throws are still awarded, and the possession then ends. **Nothing about `MAX_OFFENSIVE_REBOUNDS_PER_POSSESSION` itself is touched** (its 3→5 tuning idea stays parked in ideas.md, and #030's fence stands).

**Decision C — the possession fork is determined by WHO committed, not by which site rolled, and the four cases collapse to two rules.** The user specified four cases; enumerated against A's three sites they reduce to a single question — *was the committer on defense?*

| Committer | Shot outcome | Free throws | Possession |
|---|---|---|---|
| Defense (stopped-shot site) | shot stopped, no basket | **2**, fouled player | **offense RETAINS** (`continue`) |
| Defense (and-1 site) | **basket COUNTS** | **2**, the shooter | **offense RETAINS** (`continue`) |
| Defense (rebounding site, ~78%) | shot already missed | **2**, fouled player | **offense RETAINS** (`continue`) |
| **Offense** (rebounding site, ~22%) | shot already missed | **2**, to the **defense** | **possession ENDS** (flips) |

**Two consequences worth stating because they are where a builder will go wrong.** First, **the and-1 case is the one that visibly breaks #030 B**: the basket counts *and* two free throws are awarded *and* the offense keeps the ball — three scoring channels on one possession, which no path in the engine has ever produced. It is correct (it is the rule), and #029 B's "the and-1 never forks the possession, the make already ended it" is **explicitly superseded for the flagrant case only** — the ordinary and-1 is unchanged. Second, **the flagrant REPLACES the underlying foul's free-throw treatment, it does not add to it.** A stopped THREE is normally 3 FTs (#030 C) and an ordinary and-1 is 1 FT (#029 B); when either turns flagrant the award is **2, flat**, per the user's "always two free throws". A builder who adds 2 on top of the existing count would double-award — the single likeliest bug in this pass, and the reason the count is passed as a literal rather than derived from `shotType`.

**Decision D — the free throws are shot by THE PLAYER WHO WAS FOULED at every site, so §3.14b adds NO new shooter-selection function (user call).** Each of the three sites already holds the fouled player: the stopped-shot and and-1 sites have `shooter` in hand, and the rebounding site's fouled player comes from the existing `pickFreeThrowShooter` draw over the fouled five (#028 B, `foulDrawing`-weighted). **§3.14a's `pickTechnicalFreeThrowShooter` is deliberately NOT reused** — its premise (#032 G) is that *nobody was fouled*, so the offended team chooses its best shooter. On a flagrant somebody very much was fouled, so the technical rule's justification does not hold, and borrowing it would send flagrant FTs to the wrong player for the same reason merging the two functions was rejected in §3.14a. **The two existing rules stay two; §3.14b adds a third caller, not a third rule.** `awardFreeThrows(...)` is reused **verbatim** with `count = 2` — the fifth situation to share it since #029 D — so FT/points reconciliation is automatic.

**Decision E — flagrant-1 vs flagrant-2 is a FLAT 15% SEVERITY SUB-ROLL, taken only on a flagrant, with no causal input (user proposal, adopted).** The user's proposal, adopted directly: *if a flagrant occurs make a random roll; there is a 15% chance it is a flagrant-2, and then the player is out of the game.* This answers todo.md's question 3 — **one mechanic with a severity sub-roll, not two independently-rated mechanics.** The grade changes **nothing but ejection**: two free throws either way (the user's "always two"), the same possession fork (C), the same personal foul. **A second, independently-tuned flagrant-2 rate was rejected on measurability**: at ~33 flagrants per 102-game run a flagrant-2 line is ~5 events, which cannot be resolved at any seed count, so a second constant would be a knob nobody could ever read. A conditional share is the honest shape — it says *what fraction of flagrants are severe*, which is the thing actually known, and it needs no separate resolvability argument because it inherits the parent rate's.

**The severity roll is causally inert, exactly as the technical rate is (#032 B), and for the same structural reason** — nothing in the engine can distinguish excessive contact from ordinary contact, so weighting the grade by `foulProne` would manufacture a signal the model does not have. **The committer is already chosen** by the time this roll fires (the defender from `pickDefender`, or the rebounding-foul committer from `pickCommitter`), so unlike §3.14a there is no committer draw to make: **`foulProne` has already had its say** in who was standing there. Recorded because the symmetry with #032 C is tempting and wrong — §3.14a weighted a *selection*, this is a *grade* on a player already selected.

**Decision F — the flagrant-2 ejection is a MONOTONIC COUNTER and stays DERIVED; the #023 F stored-state exception is REFUSED A SECOND TIME, and that is this pass's most useful finding.** #031 H, roadmap.md, todo.md **and #032 F** all predicted that §3.14b is where real stored state finally becomes unavoidable — #032 F sharpened the prediction, showing the exception does not arise for technicals and asserting that "a flagrant-2 is a severity grade with no counter behind it, so it is **not derivable**". **That assertion is wrong, and the reason is E.** A flagrant-2 ejects **immediately and permanently** — one is enough, there is no accumulation threshold to remember — so the fact "this player has been ejected for a flagrant" is captured by a counter that starts at 0 and only grows: **`flagrantTwos`, with `isEjectedForFlagrant() = flagrantTwos >= 1`.** That is structurally identical to `isFouledOut()` (`fouls >= 6`) and `isEjected()` (`technicalFouls >= 2`); the limit being 1 rather than 6 or 2 changes nothing about the shape. **A stored `flagrant2` boolean would be a second source of truth for a fact the counter already holds** — precisely the #013/#015 duplicate-source trap, and exactly what #031 E refused for `inFoulTrouble` and #023 F refused for the foul-out.

**The distinction from the `inFoulTrouble` flag #031 E rejected, stated explicitly because todo.md asked for it:** #031 E refused a flag whose underlying fact was **non-monotonic** — a foul-troubled player can be benched, recover, return, and be benched again, so a stored flag would need invalidation logic and would drift. Disqualification has the opposite property: it is **absorbing**. Once true it is true for the rest of the game, so a counter can never disagree with a flag, which means the flag adds nothing but a second thing to keep in sync. **Both the #028 D-style column argument and the #033 parity argument were considered and neither applies** (H). **So §3.14b takes no exception either, and the prediction inherited from #031 H should be retired rather than carried forward again** — it was made three times, and both times it came due the mechanic turned out to be a counter. The honest reading is that the *shape* of a disqualification (absorbing, monotonic, per-player) is what makes derivation work, and every disqualification the model has is that shape.

**It extends `isDisqualified(p)` — the seam §3.14a built — to `isFouledOut() || isEjected() || isEjectedForFlagrant()`**, a one-line change behind the single `eligible(...)` filter, and **NOT a fourth removal path** (#031 H, held for the third pass running). **Unlike §3.14a's, this ejection is observable**: at ~0.16 flagrants × 15% it lands around **0.024 per team per game** — roughly *double* §3.14a's measured 0.014, so §3.14b is the pass where the hard tier is genuinely exercised, as #032 F predicted it would be. Still far too rare to tune against; forced-counter unit tests remain the validation (#032 F's discipline).

**Decision G — the rate is ~0.16 flagrants per team per game, stored as the PER-TEAM-PER-GAME figure and divided down by the FOUL count (user call; #032 B2's shape, with a different divisor).** `SimConfig` stores **`FLAGRANT_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME ≈ 0.16`** — the number a tuner reasons about and compares to calibration.md directly — and derives the per-foul probability by dividing by the personal-foul rate (~19.0/team/game, #032's landing minus its technicals), giving **~0.0084**. **The divisor differs from #032 B2's in kind and the difference matters**: a technical is rolled per *rotation check* against a nominal constant, so its divisor is a config-derived count and drifts with pace. **A flagrant is rolled per *foul*, and the foul rate is an emergent, calibrated quantity** — so the divisor is a **measured** figure that a future phase could move without touching this constant. Recorded as the honest cost: §3.16, or any pass that changes the foul rate, **silently changes the flagrant rate too** unless the divisor is revisited. That is the correct behavior directionally (more fouls, more chances for one to be excessive) but it is a coupling #032 B2's version does not have, and it must be stated rather than discovered.

**The clamp is `clampRareProbability` (#032 H's fifth site — one call, not a fourth copy), and the floor argument is quantitative but NOT as extreme as §3.14a's**: `PROB_FLOOR` (0.02) is **~2.4×** the per-foul rate, against §3.14a's >10×. Still decisive — flooring would inflate flagrants by 2.4× and make the constant tunable only upward, the #028 trap — but the margin is thinner, so the choice is worth stating rather than assuming. **The 15% severity share (E) needs no clamp at all**: it is a fixed conditional share, not a contested probability.

**Decision H — the rate is a `ballpark`, gets its OWN harness line separate from §3.14a's, and NO new box-score column (user call on the first two; H's third part reverses nothing).** **Separate instrument, separate constant (user call, and correct):** technicals and flagrants share nothing but the word "foul" (#032 A) — sharing a harness line would make one number unreadable without the other, and the two cannot even be judged at the same confidence. **Resolvability, computed before the rate was chosen** (#032 J's method, the §3.13 measure-first lesson): at 102 games ~33 events, relative sd **17.4%**; at 5 seeds ~166 events, **7.8%**. **That is materially coarser than the technicals line (11.8% / 5.3%) — a single-seed reading is useless, and even the 5-seed mean can only confirm the right order of magnitude, not a 10% tuning move.** The harness gains a **flagrants/team/game line plus a flagrant-2 count**, and the flagrant-2 ejections join §3.14a's existing ejection line rather than starting a third (an ejection is an ejection; the cause is recoverable from the event log). It enters calibration.md as a **`ballpark` (~0.25–0.40 league-wide, ~0.16 per team)**, explicitly **not** a TARGET — nothing is tuned toward it, the constant is set from the figure directly — and, like every row there, **UNSOURCED**, inheriting §3.16's job (1).

**No new box-score column, and the reason is that #033's parity argument does not reach here.** #033 surfaced `technicalFouls` because it was the **twelfth** per-player accumulator in a set whose other eleven were exposed — omitting one made the box score inconsistent. **A flagrant is a personal foul (Decision I) and therefore already counted in the existing `fouls` column**, so there is no missing member and no asymmetry: the set stays complete without a new field. The flagrant count remains queryable from the event log with one `WHERE` (#020), exactly as it was for technicals before #033. **Adding `flagrant_fouls` would be fabricating a column ahead of its consumer** (#014/#017) with none of the set-completeness cover #033 B relied on. Parked, with the trigger named: if a Phase-4 consumer wants it, it is one changeset.

**Decision I — a flagrant IS a personal foul: it uses `recordFoul()`, feeds the six-foul limit, and COUNTS toward the period bonus tally — §3.14a's counter split does NOT apply (user call, and the NBA rule).** The contrast with #032 E is total and deliberate. A technical got its own counter because it does not count toward the six; **a flagrant does**, so the committer wears it through the ordinary `recordFoul()` path and it feeds `isFouledOut()`, `foulTroubleLevel()` and §3.13's foul-trouble sit curve exactly like any other foul. **A player can therefore foul out on a flagrant, and can be sat by the §3.13 soft rule for accumulating one** — both correct, both automatic, neither requiring any code. **`flagrantTwos` (F) is NOT a parallel foul counter** and must not be mistaken for one: it counts *ejection causes*, not fouls, and the flagrant itself is already in `fouls`. Summing them would double-count — the same trap #033 D wrote into the schema comments for `fouls`/`technical_fouls`, in the opposite direction.

**The bonus tally needs NO change, and that must be deliberate rather than accidental (the §3.14a execution finding, #032's follow-up).** `GameData.countsTowardBonus(e)` excludes exactly one outcome, `TECHNICAL_FOUL`; a flagrant is not excluded, so it counts — which is the rule, and is what the existing code already does for any unrecognised `FOUL` outcome. **The trap is that the exclusion lives in TWO independent derivations** — `GameData.isInBonus` and `CalibrationHarness`'s own per-team-period tally — and §3.14a's execution found that a foul type mishandled in one but not the other makes the instrument silently disagree with the engine. **§3.14b touches neither, and the execution plan asserts that as a test rather than leaving it to inspection.**

**Decision J — the points cost is budgeted at +0.43/team/game across TWO channels, and #032 I's inverted stop condition applies again — but retention is re-priced, not assumed free.** The #030 E / #031 G / #032 I discipline requires pricing before building. At the engine's shipped numbers (~1.17 points/possession, ~75% FT, 0.16 flagrants/team/game):

| Channel | pts/team/game | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Flagrant FTs (0.16 × 2 FT × ~0.75) | **+0.24** | Net of what the underlying foul would have awarded anyway — see below |
| Retention (0.16 × ~1.17 pts/poss) | **+0.19** | **Upper bound**: assumes *every* flagrant is defensive and retained |
| **Total** | **+0.43** | vs. a ±1.5 per-seed noise band (#029 E) |

**Two corrections to the naive figures, both making the true cost lower than the table.** First, the FT channel is **gross, not net**: on the stopped-shot site the underlying foul already awarded 2 FTs (3 on a three), so a flagrant there adds **zero** free throws and on a stopped three actually *removes* one (C); on the and-1 site it adds exactly 1. The net FT cost is therefore **well under +0.24**. Second, the retention upper bound assumes no flagrant is ever refused by the cap (B) and that every one is defensive — the offensive ~22% slice of the rebounding site flips the possession instead, which is points-**negative** for the offense. **So +0.43 is a deliberate over-estimate and the real figure is likely nearer +0.3.**

**The stop condition therefore INVERTS, as it did in §3.14a: if the §3.4 aggregates move measurably at 5 seeds, that is a BUG, not a calibration result.** The likely bugs are named so the harness can be read against them: **double-awarded free throws** (C's "replaces, does not add"), **an uncapped retention loop** (B), or **a flagrant leaking out of the bonus tally** (I). **But retention is a genuinely different kind of channel from anything §3.14a had, and #028's implementation note is the cautionary precedent** — §3.10 priced its bonus FTs and found the *bigger* channel was retained possessions, which its design had not anticipated. §3.14b's retention is priced here explicitly for that reason; the difference from §3.10 is one of scale (a rebounding foul fires ~1.3 times per team-game, a flagrant 0.16), which is why the same channel is sub-noise here and was not there. **§3.14a's precedent also stands as a warning in the other direction**: its +0.26 budget landed as an observed **+0.5**, still inside the band but nearly double. A §3.14b landing near +0.9 would still be inside the band and still not be evidence of a bug; a landing beyond ±1.5 is. **§3.16 owns re-centering; §3.14b re-centers nothing and must not** — the points and FG% targets are CONTESTED (calibration.md).

**Rationale**: *(A)* Layering on top of a resolved outcome rather than carving out of it is what makes the pass free-by-construction on every existing rate, and covering all three sites is what makes C's four cases emergent rather than hand-enumerated — a subset would have required special-casing the very case the user specified. One constant across three sites because the sites differ in what the foul was, not in how likely it was excessive, and three constants would be unreadable at 33 events. *(B)* The loop already implements "same team, run it again", so the invariant #030 B named is broken by *reaching* existing machinery rather than by building new machinery — which is why the crux of §3.14b turns out to be five lines. The cap is the loop's only bound and every other retention path respects it; exempting the rarest path from the only guard would be backwards. *(C)* Reducing four specified cases to one question (was the committer on defense?) is what keeps the fork honest; and stating the replace-don't-add rule explicitly is warranted because #030 C and #029 B both made the FT count situation-dependent, so a builder has two live precedents for deriving it. *(D)* The fouled player shoots is the rule, every site already holds him, and §3.14a's technical rule rests on a premise ("nobody was fouled") that is false here — so reusing it would repeat the exact merge #032 G rejected. *(E)* A conditional share expresses the thing actually known and inherits the parent rate's resolvability; a second independent rate would be a knob nobody could read at ~5 events per run. The grade is inert because the engine cannot tell excessive contact from ordinary contact, and the committer selection has already had `foulProne`'s input. *(F)* An immediate ejection has no accumulation to remember, so the fact is absorbing and monotonic — the exact shape #023 F's derivation was built for — and a flag would be a second source of truth for something a counter already holds (#013/#015). Refusing the exception a second time is more useful than inheriting a prediction that has now been wrong twice. *(G)* A game-level constant beats an uninterpretable per-foul probability (#032 B2), and naming the divisor's emergent nature is what stops a future foul-rate change from silently moving flagrants unnoticed. *(H)* Two mechanics that share nothing (#032 A) cannot share one instrument, and computing resolvability before choosing the rate is the §3.13 lesson; the no-column call keeps #033's parity argument confined to the case that actually earned it, rather than letting it become a general licence. *(I)* Following the real rule costs nothing here because `recordFoul()` is the ordinary path — the work is entirely in *not* copying §3.14a — and asserting the untouched bonus tally as a test is the only way "we changed nothing" is distinguishable from "we forgot". *(J)* Pricing first is #030 E / #031 G / #032 I discipline; pricing retention **separately and as an upper bound** is the direct lesson of #028's surprise, and stating that the budget is deliberately over-estimated stops a smaller landing from reading as a missing mechanic.  
**Trade-off**: *(A)* Three call sites for one roll means three places to get the plumbing wrong, and the shared constant means a future finding that (say) rebounding fouls turn flagrant at a different rate than shooting fouls cannot be expressed without splitting it. *(B)* The retention path makes `resolvePossession` measurably harder to read — it now has **five** ways to re-enter the loop (offensive rebound, block recovery, OOB-offense, rebounding foul, flagrant), and a sixth would be a genuine signal that the loop wants restructuring. Counting a flagrant against a constant named `MAX_OFFENSIVE_REBOUNDS_PER_POSSESSION` deepens an already-stale name (§3.7 and §3.8 got there first), and the constant now bounds four non-rebound paths. *(C)* The and-1 flagrant produces three scoring channels on one possession, which will look like a double-count bug to every future reader of a play-by-play; and the replace-don't-add rule means a stopped flagrant three awards **fewer** free throws than the ordinary foul it upgraded — correct by rule, but counter-intuitive enough to invite a "fix". *(D)* The rebounding-site flagrant's shooter comes from a `foulDrawing`-weighted draw, so *who* was flagrantly fouled on a board is decided by a skill that models drawing ordinary contact — a small fiction, accepted because the alternative is a new selection rule for ~0.03 events per team-game. *(E)* The severity grade carries **no player character whatsoever** — a disciplined veteran and a reckless rookie are equally likely to commit a flagrant-2 given a flagrant, which is a real fidelity ceiling no tuning can lift (the same ceiling #032 B accepted for technicals). *(F)* Refusing stored state for the third time means the "§3.14 needs real stored state" thread that has run through #031 H, roadmap.md and todo.md ends with nothing built — a reader tracing that prediction forward will find only refusals, and the entry has to explain why rather than point at code. *(G)* The rate is coupled to the emergent foul rate, so §3.16 can move flagrants without intending to; and the ~2.4× floor margin is thin enough that a future rate increase could bring the constant near `PROB_FLOOR` territory where the floor-free choice stops being obviously right. *(H)* A second rare-event harness line that can only be read at 5 seeds, and even then coarsely — the instrument can confirm the roll fires at roughly the right rate and essentially nothing more. Refusing the column means the one thing §3.14b adds to a player's game is invisible to API clients, which is defensible but will read as an inconsistency to anyone who saw #033 land the opposite way one session earlier. *(I)* Flagrants feeding the foul-out and foul-trouble machinery means §3.14b can move §3.13's calibrated foul-out number — at 0.16 fouls/team/game the effect is far below its 0.368–0.441 seed spread, but it is not structurally zero the way §3.14a's was. *(J)* A sub-noise, two-channel cost means the aggregates cannot demonstrate correctness — absence of movement is consistent with "correct" and with "the roll never fires" — so H's instrument is load-bearing exactly as #032 J's was, and the over-estimated budget makes any landing hard to falsify.  
**Alternatives considered**: *(A)* **A flagrant as its own foul roll, independent of the existing ones** — rejected: it would be a fifth foul rate competing with four calibrated ones, and it contradicts the user's "a flagrant requires a foul to have occurred". **Only the stopped-shot site can turn flagrant** — rejected: it drops the offensive-flagrant case the user explicitly specified (only the rebounding site can produce it) and ~30% of the foul volume. **Three per-site rates** — rejected: unresolvable at ~11 events per site per run. *(B)* **A new fork/branch outside the second-chance loop** — rejected: the loop already *is* the retention mechanism, and a parallel path would be a second way to express one idea (#013/#015). **Exempt the flagrant from `MAX_OFFENSIVE_REBOUNDS_PER_POSSESSION`** — rejected: the cap is the loop's only bound, the exemption would make the rarest path the only unbounded one, and the case it would change arises roughly once every several games. **Raise the cap to accommodate it** — rejected: that is the parked 3→5 tuning idea (ideas.md), which needs its own recalibration pass and must not ride in on an unrelated phase. *(C)* **Award the flagrant's 2 FTs IN ADDITION to the underlying foul's** — rejected: it is not the rule (the flagrant replaces the ordinary award) and would double-count, the likeliest bug in this pass. **Derive the count from `shotType` like #030 C does** — rejected: a flagrant is 2 flat by rule, and deriving it is precisely how the count would silently graduate. *(D)* **Reuse `pickTechnicalFreeThrowShooter` (the best shooter on the floor)** — rejected: its premise is that nobody was fouled (#032 G); on a flagrant somebody was, so it would send the FTs to the wrong player. **A new flagrant-specific shooter rule** — rejected: fabricating a third selection rule when both existing sites already hold the fouled player. *(E)* **Two independently-rated mechanics (a flagrant-1 rate and a flagrant-2 rate)** — rejected: ~5 flagrant-2s per 102-game run is unresolvable at any seed count, so the second constant would be untunable. **Weight the severity roll by `foulProne`** — rejected: the engine cannot distinguish excessive from ordinary contact, and `foulProne` already decided *who* was there — weighting the grade too would apply one signal twice. **A single grade with no ejection at all** — rejected (user call): the ejection is the point of modelling the grade, and it is the seam §3.14a built for it. *(F)* **A stored `flagrant2` / `ejected` boolean on `PlayerGameState`** — rejected: a second source of truth for a fact the monotonic counter already holds, the #013/#015 trap that #023 F and #031 E both refused; the prediction that §3.14b would force it (#031 H, #032 F) is simply wrong, because an immediate ejection has no accumulation to remember. **A dedicated `EJECTION` event or a fourth removal path in `RotationState`** — rejected pre-emptively by #031 H, held for the third pass: disqualification is disqualification, and it extends `isDisqualified(...)`. **Reuse `technicalFouls` as a shared "ejection counter"** — rejected: it would make `getTechnicalFouls()` (now an exposed box-score column, #033) report flagrants, silently corrupting a surfaced stat. *(G)* **Store the per-foul probability (~0.0084) directly** — rejected: uninterpretable, not comparable to calibration.md, and it would drift silently if the foul rate moved (#032 B2's argument). **Divide by possessions rather than fouls** — rejected: the roll fires per foul, so a possession divisor would misstate the relationship and break the moment the foul rate moved. **Use `clampProbability` (with the floor)** — rejected quantitatively: `PROB_FLOOR` is ~2.4× the rate, so it would inflate flagrants and make the constant tunable only upward (#028's trap). *(H)* **Share §3.14a's technicals harness line** — rejected (user call): the two mechanics share nothing (#032 A), the numbers have different resolvability, and one line would make neither readable. **Promote the rate to a TARGET** — rejected: nothing is tuned toward it, which is not what calibration.md means by TARGET. **Add a `flagrant_fouls` box-score column on #033's parity argument** — rejected: a flagrant is already inside the existing `fouls` column, so there is no missing member of the set and #033 B's argument has no purchase; it would be fabricating ahead of a consumer (#014/#017). *(I)* **Give flagrants their own counter, mirroring §3.14a** — rejected: it is the opposite of the rule, and it would remove flagrants from the six-foul limit and the period bonus tally, breaking both. **Exclude flagrants from the bonus tally** — rejected: not the rule, and it would require the two-place exclusion (#032's follow-up) for no reason. *(J)* **Budget a re-centering pass for the added points** — rejected: at +0.43 (over-estimated) against ±1.5 noise there is nothing to re-center, and §3.16 owns the aggregates.  
**Status of §3.14b decisions**: A–J all resolved by this entry. Net schema change: **NONE** — no new column, no OpenAPI change, no new `PlayType`, no new box-score field (H), no new coach attribute (#018's five stand), no new player attribute or skill. **Two new `outcome` strings on the existing `PlayType.FOUL`** — free text since #020, so no migration; spellings **`FLAGRANT_FOUL_1`** and **`FLAGRANT_FOUL_2`**, mirroring the `SHOOTING_FOUL` / `AND_ONE` / `TECHNICAL_FOUL` vocabulary and colliding with nothing in the §3.8/§3.9/§3.10 strings (the #027 D discipline). The grade rides the outcome suffix, so no separate field carries it. **One new `FreeThrowSource.FLAGRANT`** — the same argument that forced §3.14a's divergence: the harness reads FT source off the outcome suffix (#029 D), so folding flagrant FTs into `SHOOTING` would silently inflate a real source's share on the line §3.14b is judged by, and it would be factually wrong on the rebounding site (not a shooting foul at all). **`committingTeamId` is populated** like every other `FOUL` event (#028 D) — and here it is load-bearing rather than uniform, since C's fork depends on which side committed. New engine pieces: `SimConfig.FLAGRANT_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME` + `FLAGRANT_TWO_SHARE` + a per-foul derivation helper (G) routed through `clampRareProbability` (#032 H's **fifth** site — one call, not a fourth copy); a `flagrantTwos` counter + `recordFlagrantTwo()` + a **derived** `isEjectedForFlagrant()` on `PlayerGameState` (F); `isDisqualified(...)` extended to a third cause (F, #031 H); the severity roll in `FoulResolver` (A/E); the retention fork in `PossessionEngine.resolvePossession` reusing the existing `continue` under the existing cap (B/C); `awardFreeThrows(..., count = 2, FreeThrowSource.FLAGRANT)` at three call sites (D). `CalibrationHarness` gains a flagrants + flagrant-2 line, and flagrant ejections join the existing ejection line (H). **`possession-flow.puml` DOES change** — unlike §3.14a (#032 D), this pass adds a real branch on the possession path at all three foul sites, plus the first FT-then-`continue` edge in the diagram; it is drawn in this design session per the project-docs rhythm. **Determinism**: the severity roll adds **one `nextDouble()` per FOUL** (not per possession) at each of the three sites, and the flagrant-2 sub-roll adds a second **only on a hit** — a deliberate exception to §3.14a's unconditional-draw discipline, permissible because it is nested *inside* an already-conditional branch (the foul), so it cannot fork the stream on rotation state the way #031's would have; it forks only on the foul's own outcome, which the stream already forked on. Every seed-pinned expectation downstream of a foul re-baselines once. **Open-at-execution (small, constrained)**: the exact `FLAGRANT_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME` value (constrained: back-solved from the ~0.25–0.40 league figure and confirmed against H's new harness line at 5 seeds); whether the three call sites share one `FoulResolver.isFlagrant(...)` helper or the roll is taken in `PossessionEngine` beside each award (constrained: one roll definition, three callers, never three copies); the exact divisor expression for G's derivation (constrained: the personal-foul rate, with the emergent-coupling comment); and whether the flagrant-2 line is worth printing separately once it reads ~0.02 (H). **Not open**: the layered-roll shape and three-site coverage (A), retention as the existing `continue` under the existing cap (B), the committer-determined fork and replace-don't-add FT count (C), the fouled player shoots (D), the 15% flat severity share (E), the ejection stays **derived** with no stored state (F), the game-level constant shape (G), the separate instrument and no new column (H), `recordFoul()` and the untouched bonus tally (I), and the no-re-centering fence (J).

**Implementation note (from execution, 2026-08).** Shipped as A–J specify, with **no design divergence** — the possession-retention fork was the predicted five lines (`offensiveRebounds++; continue;` under the existing cap) at all three sites, and F's refusal of stored state held: `flagrantTwos` + a derived `isEjectedForFlagrant()`, `isDisqualified(...)` extended to a third cause, no flag, no fourth removal path. **Resolved the open-at-execution items**: `FLAGRANT_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME = 0.16`; the three sites share **one** `FoulResolver.isFlagrant(rng)` plus one `isFlagrantTwo(rng)`, both skill-free, with the emit-and-award plumbing shared by a single `PossessionEngine.awardFlagrant(...)` (three callers, one definition); the divisor is the **named** `PERSONAL_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME = 19.0` carrying G's emergent-coupling javadoc; the flagrant-2 line **is** printed separately (it reads ~0.02 and drives the ejections line, so it earns its row). **Four execution findings worth recording.** (1) **`FLAGRANT_EJECTION_LIMIT = 1` was added rather than inlining `>= 1`** — it makes the third disqualification threshold read identically to `FOUL_OUT_LIMIT` and `TECHNICAL_EJECTION_LIMIT`, which is F's whole point. (2) **A flagrant REPLACES the underlying foul's EVENT, not just its free throws** — so `Fouls / team / game` does **not** rise (measured 19.35 against §3.14a's ~19.4), unlike §3.14a's technical which added an event. The harness caveat comment was corrected accordingly; the design's "subtract both" wording would have been wrong. (3) **`capReached` was hoisted to the top of the `resolvePossession` loop body** (the preferred option), a pure move that also removed the pre-existing duplicate pair at the block-recovery and rebound sites. (4) **`PossessionEngineTest.scoringTeamId` was generalized from a suffix list to the actual rule** — "the FTs score for whoever did NOT commit the foul". §3.14b's RNG shift exposed a **latent §3.14a bug in that test helper**: a `MADE_TECHNICAL` free throw shot by a defender during the offending team's own offensive possession was being attributed to the wrong team. The engine was always right (`awardFreeThrows` takes an explicit `shootingTeamId`); the helper could not tell. Three sources needing the exception in three phases was the signal to stop enumerating. **One seed re-baselined**, by re-derivation not by pasting: `GameSimulatorIntegrationTest`'s technicals test moved 7L → 9L, because its `> 0` line is a *precondition* keeping the reconciliation non-vacuous and the seed-7 stream no longer happens to draw a technical; seed 9 was chosen by measuring which seeds still do (it yields three). `RotationStateTest` needed **no** re-baselining, as A predicted — its draw-count test stays 3. **Landing (5-seed mean, 102 games each): flagrants 0.148/team/game** (against the 0.16 configured — inside the 7.8% relative sd this line resolves at, and the most it can confirm), **flagrant-2s 0.023**, **ejections 0.027** (against F's predicted ~0.024 — the hard tier is now genuinely exercised, roughly double §3.14a's 0.014). **§3.4 aggregates: 118.3 pts / 46.9% FG / 36.7% 3P / 27.1 ast / 13.6 TO**, against §3.14a's 117.5 / 46.6 / 37.0 / 26.8 / 13.5 — **+0.76 points**, inside the ±1.5 band and between J's over-estimated +0.43 budget and the "§3.14a landed at nearly double its budget" caution, so **not** evidence of a bug. **None of J's three named signatures are present**: FT/points reconciliation is exact (the guard-tests assert 2 FTs at all three sites), the retention loop stops at the cap (asserted at all three sites), and the bonus tally is unmoved (51.9% of team-periods against 51.2%, with a test pinning that `isInBonus` counts a flagrant). Foul-outs 0.358 (§3.13's 0.409), untouched. **Tests: 513 → 542**; `sim` package coverage back to **99.2%** line, full `mvn clean install` gate green.

**§3.14b follow-up (carry forward)**:
- **The "§3.14 needs real stored state" prediction is now RETIRED, not deferred** (F). #031 H, roadmap.md and todo.md all carried it; #032 F narrowed it to flagrant-2; this entry shows flagrant-2 is a monotonic counter too. **Every disqualification in the model is absorbing, monotonic and per-player, which is exactly the shape #023 F's derivation was built for** — so the exception should not be predicted again without a mechanic that is genuinely non-monotonic. If one ever appears, #031 E's `inFoulTrouble` reasoning is the test to apply.
- **A flagrant has no causal input beyond who was already selected** (A/E's accepted ceiling) — no temperament, no game situation, no score margin, and the flagrant-2 grade is pure chance. Same missing prerequisite as §3.14a's technicals and every parked strategic-sub idea: game-situation awareness (#031's follow-up, ideas.md).
- **`resolvePossession` now has FIVE loop re-entry paths** (B) — offensive rebound, block recovery, OOB-offense, rebounding foul, flagrant. **A sixth is the signal to restructure the loop**, in the same spirit as #030's clamp-helper trigger. **Both this trigger and the rename below are now tracked as a single behavior-free entry in [ideas.md](ideas.md)** (raised 2026-08 from reading the diagram, which is a 1:1 mirror of the method), kept deliberately separate from the 3→5 tuning idea because neither changes a number. Also: the cap bounds four non-rebound paths, so **the rename this bullet proposed was DONE in 2026-08**, immediately after §3.14b's close-out rather than waiting for a pass to touch it — `MAX_OFFENSIVE_REBOUNDS_PER_POSSESSION` → **`MAX_OFFENSIVE_RETENTIONS_PER_POSSESSION`**, with the loop counter `offensiveRebounds` → `offensiveRetentions`. A **pure rename**: value unchanged at 3, 542 tests green, and the 5-seed landing reproduced **byte-for-byte** (so §3.15's validation gate is undisturbed). `PlayerGameState.offensiveRebounds` — the box-score stat — is a different thing and was deliberately untouched. The **loop restructure** remains parked on its sixth-path trigger.
- **The flagrant rate is coupled to the emergent FOUL rate** (G), unlike §3.14a's pace-coupled technical rate. **§3.16, or any pass that moves the foul rate, moves flagrants too** without touching `FLAGRANT_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME`. Directionally correct, but it means the constant is not a standalone dial.
- **No `flagrant_fouls` box-score column** (H). Deliberately parked: a flagrant is already inside `fouls`, so #033's parity argument does not reach it, and the count is one `WHERE` over the event log (#020). One changeset whenever a Phase-4 consumer wants it — the same shape #033 took.
- **Ejections still have no event and no column** (#033's follow-up, unchanged). They are now derivable from **two** causes — `TECHNICAL_FOUL` count vs `TECHNICAL_EJECTION_LIMIT`, and any `FLAGRANT_FOUL_2` — which makes "was this player ejected, and why" a two-query question. If a consumer ever wants ejections, that plurality is the argument for a single `EJECTION` event that #033's follow-up anticipated.
- **The flagrant rate joins calibration.md as an UNSOURCED ballpark** (H), inheriting §3.16's job (1). Judged **only at 5 seeds**, and even then coarsely (17.4% relative sd at 102 games, 7.8% at 5 seeds) — **the coarsest row in the table.**
- **§3.14b is the last new mechanic in Phase 3.** §3.15 (profiles) validates by reproducing §3.14b's landing exactly; §3.16 recalibrates against verified targets and owns the CONTESTED points/FG% pair. Neither adds a possession branch — **`possession-flow.puml` is structurally complete after this pass.**
- (The §3.7-E shot-clock-pressure, §3.9 finer turnover sub-types, §3.10 observed-split and `committing_team_id`-on-`GameEvent`, §3.12 3PA-volume, §3.13 saturation/time-awareness/`substitutionAggressiveness`-split, §3.14a bench-and-coach-technicals, and §3.6 time-column follow-ups all still stand.)

---

### 035 — `SimConfig` profiles (§3.15): the 57 tunable values move OUT to an external `baseline.properties` that becomes their ONLY copy, the constants become INSTANCE state because a profile must eventually be a per-simulation input, only rules + model machinery stay `static final`, and calibration targets are declared BASELINE-ONLY so an era profile reads as a comparison rather than a wall of misses
**Date**: 2026-08  
**Scope**: §3.15 is the **first non-mechanic sub-phase in the arc** — **no possession branch, no event, no rate, no `PlayType`, no `FreeThrowSource`, no counter**. `possession-flow.puml` is structurally complete after #034 and **must not change in this pass**; a design that starts wanting a new branch has gone wrong. It changes **how the constants are loaded**, so tuning becomes *comparable experiments* rather than sequential edits. **Engine + harness only: no schema change, no OpenAPI change, no persistence** — but see B, which builds the substrate for a Phase-4 per-game profile deliberately rather than incidentally. **§3.15 changes no number**; its gate is reproducing §3.14b's landing byte-for-byte on the baseline profile (G). NOT YET BUILT — the execute-ready plan is todo.md's §3.15 execution plan.

**Decision A — the tunable values move OUT to external config: `application-baseline.properties` carries all 57 and is their ONLY copy; `SimConfig.java` holds no numbers.** `private final double baseThree;` — no initializer. **`baseline` is a full replacement** (every value present, so one file is the complete effective config); **era profiles are deltas on top of it** ("baseline except these"), which falls out of Spring's own later-profile-wins override order rather than needing to be built. Keys are **kebab-case** (`sim.base-no-basket-foul`), Spring's recommended format, which relaxed binding maps to the `baseThree` field. *(Kebab is a convention stated in the file header, **not enforced in code** — relaxed binding accepts camel or underscore too and applies them correctly, so a non-kebab key is a style inconsistency, not a wrong run. Failing a build over it would guard a non-problem.)*

**⚠ No Java initializers is the load-bearing part, not a detail.** With them the 57 numbers would exist **twice** — once in the file, once in the field — and the 542 tests, which construct `SimConfig` directly, would keep passing against the Java copy while the file drifted. Two sources of truth for a calibrated number is the trap refused elsewhere (#013/#015, #023 F); with none, drift is not caught-by-a-test but **impossible**. Consequences: `SimConfig` cannot be constructed without the file, so `new SimConfig()` becomes a loader-backed **`SimConfig.baseline()`**, and **the completeness check is structural** — a constant missing from the file has no value at all (D).

**⚠ `baseline` is a REFERENCE POINT, not a playstyle.** It is the datum the others are expressed relative to: what the gate reproduces (G) and what every `calibration.md` target refers to (I). **Changing playstyle means running a different profile, never editing baseline** — that would move the reference and forfeit the claim that the engine still reproduces §3.14b. Baseline is also, unavoidably, the *modern* era (everything is tuned toward modern-NBA figures), so **§3.15 ships `baseline` + `nineties` only**; a `modern.properties` would be a near-duplicate unless it claimed something baseline does not — the real candidate being the ~20 vs ~35 3PA/team/game gap (#030's follow-up).

**These ARE Spring profiles, COMPOSED with the existing infrastructure ones — `spring.profiles.active=local,baseline` (user call).** The two axes are orthogonal and Spring already composes them: `local`/`test` answer *which database*, `baseline`/`nineties` answer *which basketball*. So `local,baseline` is "Postgres + modern", `test,nineties` is "H2 + 90s", and switching eras is a startup property rather than a code path. **`application-baseline.properties` holds `sim.*` keys only**, bound by **`@ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "sim")`** — no custom loader, no new flag, no hand-rolled parsing, and type conversion and binding come free.

**This deliberately replaces an earlier design in this same pass, and the rejected version is worth naming because its objection was wrong.** A custom loader keyed on a new `-DcalibrationProfile=<name>` flag was specified first, on the reasoning that a sim profile "would displace `local` and leave no datasource". **That is only true of a single active profile; with a comma list it is simply false** — composition is what profile lists are for. The custom loader survived only to keep `SimConfig` per-*instance* (two profiles live in one JVM), and **that capability has no consumer**: the harness runs one profile per invocation, and a Phase-4 era feature may well want per-*deployment* eras anyway. Building it now would be fabricating ahead of a consumer (#014/#017). **Spring profiles resolve once per JVM, which is the accepted cost** — recorded plainly so Phase 4 knows what to revisit if it ever genuinely needs per-game config.

**⚠ BASELINE IS ALWAYS PRESENT, AND ORDER MATTERS — the one sharp edge of the delta shape.** Because an era file lists only its deltas, **it cannot be loaded instead of baseline**: `test,nineties` alone would leave ~50 properties unbound and fail the context at startup. The correct combinations are **`local,baseline`** (modern) and **`local,baseline,nineties`** (90s). Within that list Spring's rule is **later wins** — the same rule that already makes `application-local.properties` override `application.properties` — so a key in both files takes the era file's value. **The failure mode is that this is POSITIONAL, not semantic**: `local,nineties,baseline` puts baseline last, so it wins and the era profile silently does nothing, with no error. That is exactly what F's effective-config dump catches — a run that prints `sim.base-three=0.3375` when the operator believed they were running the 90s is immediately visible — and it is a second reason that dump is load-bearing rather than decorative. **Convention: never stack two ERA profiles** (`baseline,nineties,eighties`); one era on top of baseline, or baseline alone.

**Two further consequences of using the real mechanism.** *(i)* **The tests have no `@ActiveProfiles`** — they inherit `spring.profiles.active` from `application.properties` — so adding `baseline` there means every `@SpringBootTest` loads it. That is correct (the suite should run the shipped config) but it makes the file part of the standard test context rather than harness-only. *(ii)* Nothing structurally stops an era file setting `spring.datasource.*`; that is a discipline point, not a mechanism, and it is the one thing the rejected custom loader would have prevented.

**The reasoning stays in the Java; the file carries pointers.** `SimConfig` holds ~640 comment lines of traps that have repeatedly stopped real mistakes (#028's wrong-way lever, the `PROB_FLOOR` trap, #030's "multipliers, not probabilities", #031's saturated curve). **§3.15 neither moves nor trims that prose** — `baseline.properties` gets a 1–2 line pointer per value (the trap, the units warning, the `#NNN`) so the warning sits where the edit happens. **Trimming the javadoc is folded into the Phase-4 condense pass**: the verbosity exists largely *because* the javadoc restates `decisions.md`, making them one job, and that pass runs after §3.16, its heaviest consumer. The file is **grouped by what the knob does** — pace (2), shooting (5), fouls & FTs (9), rebounding & loose balls (16), turnovers (10), fatigue & rotation (12), rare events (3) — not by the phase that added it, because authoring an era profile means finding "all the foul knobs", not "the §3.10 knobs". Its header states what is **not** in it, so a reader hunting `PROB_FLOOR` learns it is machinery rather than assuming the file is incomplete.

**Decision B — the constants become INSTANCE state on the existing `SimConfig` bean, NOT static-mutable, because a profile must eventually be a PER-SIMULATION input (user intent, and the crux of the pass).** This is todo.md's Q2. **The decisive fact is not the refactor's size but a compile-time rule**: a `public static final` primitive initialized from a literal is a JLS §4.12.4 *constant variable*, so **javac inlines its value into every caller's bytecode**. `SimConfig` is never consulted at runtime. That has three consequences this pass turns on — a properties file cannot override such a field at all; a "compatibility alias" left behind for tests would be **baked into each test class at its own compile time**, so a test could assert `0.3375` while the engine ran `0.31` with no error; and it is the already-recorded reason §3.11's `-DandOneBaseOverride=0` flag silently did nothing against three "baseline" runs. **Aliases were therefore rejected outright**: they reintroduce exactly one way for a run to lie, in the file most likely to be believed.

**Dropping `final` while keeping `static` also fixes the inlining, and is the cheapest possible pass** — all ~323 call sites would compile untouched. **It was rejected on the stated goal: eventually running *games* under a different profile.** Games simulate through `GametimeServiceImp` → `GameSimulator` in a long-lived Spring app with concurrent requests, where process-global mutable config cannot express which profile a given simulation uses — two concurrent games would read each other's values mid-possession, and a static field has no caller to ask. **Static-mutable is not a cheaper version of the target design; it cannot express it.** It also leaves `public static` fields writable from anywhere, so a test that sets one and does not reset it corrupts every later test in that JVM.

**So `SimConfig` stops being a bag of constants and becomes an object a simulation is RUN WITH.** It is already a `@Component` injected in 17 places for its 20 instance methods, and **9 of the 12 main-code readers already hold an injected `SimConfig config` field** (`FoulResolver`, `TurnoverResolver`, `ShotResolver`, `BlockResolver`, `ReboundResolver`, `PossessionEngine`, `GameSimulator`, `RotationState`, `CoachModifiers`) — for those, `SimConfig.BASE_THREE` → `config.baseThree()` is mechanical and local. **Three readers do not and must be threaded**: `PlayerGameState` (17 static reads, a `new`-per-player value object constructed at 9 sites), `GameData` (`BONUS_FOULS_PER_PERIOD`), `MissedShotResolver` (the three `OOB_*` weights). **`GametimeServiceImp`** reads `DEFAULT_POSSESSIONS_PER_PERIOD` at one site and is already a bean. **Measured blast radius: 323 static call sites — 84 main, 239 test** (`SimConfigTest` 80, `PossessionEngineTest` 29, `FoulResolverTest` 27, `PlayerGameStateTest` 27, `RotationStateTest` 25). This is the large-pass answer, taken deliberately: it is paid once, and it is what unblocks the Phase-4 goal instead of requiring a second pass over the same code.

**Decision C — the profilable set is WIDE: 57 constants, with only RULES and MODEL MACHINERY left `static final` (user call).** **The invariant reads off the source: `static final` means it is a rule or the shape of the model, not a knob.** The 26 that stay static:

| Group | Constants | Why static |
|---|---|---|
| **Rules** (9) | `PERIODS`, `MINUTES_PER_PERIOD`, `OT_MINUTES`, `FREE_THROWS_PER_FOUL`, `AND_ONE_FREE_THROWS`, `TECHNICAL_FREE_THROWS`, `FLAGRANT_FREE_THROWS`, `TECHNICAL_EJECTION_LIMIT`, `FLAGRANT_EJECTION_LIMIT` | No era story; the game's structure and its FT/ejection arithmetic |
| **Model machinery** (16) | `SCALE_AVG`, **`MAX_ENERGY`**, `PROB_FLOOR`, `PROB_CEILING`, `SENSITIVITY`, and the per-event `*_SENSITIVITY` family (`BLOCK_`, `REBOUND_FOUL_`, `AND_ONE_`, `TO_CAUSE_`, `COACH_`, `ASSIST_`, `ACUMEN_`, `TEAM_EFFICIENCY_`, `FT_`, `ENDURANCE_DRAIN_`, `FOUL_TROUBLE_VALUE_`) | The shape of the contest model, not a property of an era. **The two SCALE DEFINITIONS are here**, and the `*_SENSITIVITY` family sets **talent spread** — see below |
| **Measured** (1) | `PERSONAL_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME` | **See below — a different kind of exclusion entirely** |

**⚠ THE TWO SCALE DEFINITIONS — `SCALE_AVG` and `MAX_ENERGY` — are static for a reason distinct from the rest, and it is the one a profile author will get wrong.** Neither is a knob; each is the **denominator that gives every other constant in its family a meaning**. `SCALE_AVG` (10.0, 28 sites) is the attribute scale — every `(attr − SCALE_AVG) / SCALE_AVG` deviation is measured against it. `MAX_ENERGY` (100.0) is the same for energy: a player starts at it, `fatigueFactor` divides by it, recovery clamps to it. **Changing either does not move behavior in the direction it appears to** — setting `MAX_ENERGY = 200` looks like "more stamina", but `ENERGY_DRAIN_PER_POSSESSION = 2.6` and `BASE_SUB_ENERGY_THRESHOLD = 62.0` are expressed **in units of that scale**, so it silently halves the effective drain and moves the sub threshold from 62% of a tank to 31%. The author would believe they turned one knob and would have rescaled three. **The knobs that honestly express "how tiring is this era" are the ones measured against the scale** — drain, recovery, `FATIGUE_MAX_PENALTY`, `BASE_SUB_ENERGY_THRESHOLD` — and all four are profilable, so nothing is lost.

**⚠ THE `*_SENSITIVITY` FAMILY IS THE TALENT-SPREAD DIAL, and is static because it is ONE knob spread across 15 constants.** A base rate says *where the league sits*; a sensitivity says *how far apart the players within it are* (`base + SENSITIVITY × (skillA − skillB)/10`). Low compresses the league, high stretches it — so "superteam era vs parity era" is a real distinction and this is the only lever expressing it, since attributes come from seed data on a fixed scale. **But turning one in isolation is the §3.7 mistake**: at the global 0.5 a good rim protector (14) vs an average finisher (10) blocked **~23%** of shots against a real ~5–6%, skill alone driving blocks 2–3× over target *regardless of base rate* — which is why `BLOCK_SENSITIVITY = 0.12` exists. A profile raising it to build a "defensive era" would not get more defense; it would break the base-dominant property the constant was created to protect. **Exposing 15 keys invites exactly that**, so the family stays static and talent spread is parked as a future single-multiplier axis ([ideas.md](ideas.md)) needing its own calibration pass.

**`PERSONAL_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME` is excluded on grounds unrelated to the other 24, and this is the trap worth not rediscovering (#034 G).** It is neither a rule nor a knob: it is a **measured description of what the engine currently does** — the divisor turning the game-level flagrant rate into a per-foul probability. **A profile that varies it independently of the actual foul rate silently breaks the flagrant rate**, and a 1990s profile raising `BASE_NO_BASKET_FOUL` is precisely the case where a tuner would be tempted to "keep it consistent" by hand. D makes the attempt a hard failure rather than a silent one.

**Two constants that look excludable are deliberately IN.** *(i)* **`MAX_OFFENSIVE_RETENTIONS_PER_POSSESSION`** — the parked 3→5 idea (#034 B / ideas.md) is fenced behind its own recalibration pass because the cap fires on the common path. **But that fence protects the BASELINE, and a profile cannot touch the baseline**; nothing is tuned against a non-baseline profile, and higher offensive-rebound rates are a genuine 1990s trait. **The parked idea still applies to the baseline value specifically** and is unaffected by this. *(ii)* **`FOUL_TROUBLE_SIT_PROBABILITY`** — the file's only non-scalar, a `double[7]`, so it is the one constant needing a list-parsing convention in a flat properties file (settled at execution). "We would have to write a comma-split" is too thin a reason to make the fatigue/rotation family arbitrarily incomplete. *(Note the curve is measured **saturated** — #031's note — so a profile moving it should expect little; that is a tuning fact, not a reason to exclude it.)*

**Decision D — a missing or unbindable value is a STARTUP FAILURE, not a warning — and with A's no-initializer rule most of this is structural rather than hand-written.** Because the fields have no defaults, **a constant absent from `baseline.properties` simply has no value**, so Spring's binder fails the context at startup naming the property. That covers the completeness case for free. Two checks are added on top: **`@Validated` constraints** on the bound type (rates in `[0,1]`, counts positive, the foul-trouble array exactly length 7), and a test asserting **every profilable field is present in `application-baseline.properties`** — the guard against someone adding a field with a `= 0.0` initializer out of habit and silently reintroducing a second source of truth. **The failure mode all of this exists to eliminate is a run that is silently not the config it claims**; §3.11 lost three runs to precisely that. *(An unknown-key check is NOT taken: Spring ignores unrecognized properties, and building a custom rejecter would mean enumerating the legal set by hand — the same duplication A just removed. `sim.personal-fouls-per-team-game` in a profile simply does nothing, which is visible in F's effective-config dump.)*

**Decision E — tests construct a BASELINE default and read it as an instance, with no static aliases (user call).** `SimConfig.baseline()` (or the no-arg constructor) yields the shipped values; the 239 test sites convert to `config.baseThree()` alongside main code. **Ten test files already do `new SimConfig()`**, so the pattern exists rather than being invented. The diff is large and mechanical, and the property that matters is that **nothing can silently disagree** — every read is a real runtime read of the same object the engine uses. This is the direct consequence of B's inlining finding: an alias would have made `SimConfigTest`'s 80 assertions authoritative-looking and wrong under any non-baseline profile.

**Decision F — the harness runs ONE PROFILE PER INVOCATION, selected by the ordinary Spring profile list, and prints the effective config (user call: "Level 1").** No new flag: the operator sets **`-Dspring.profiles.active=test,nineties`** beside the existing `-DcalibrationSeed=NNNN` (#029 E), and the default `test,baseline` means every current invocation keeps working unchanged. Each run is the existing ~102-game averaged report; the operator invokes it once per profile and compares reports — **the same manual outer loop already used for the 5-seed mean**, not a new workflow. **An in-process comparison table looping profiles was considered and deferred**: the nicer instrument, but additional harness machinery in a pass gated on byte-for-byte reproduction — and note Spring profiles resolve per JVM (A), so it would need one JVM per profile or a rebound context. The report gains a header naming **the active profile list** and an **effective-config block** enumerating every profilable constant and its value. **From §3.15 on, an implementation note names the profile alongside the seeds** — a landing without one is ambiguous once more than one profile exists.

**Decision G — the validation gate binds the BASELINE profile, and the determinism hazard is a DRAW-COUNT hazard, not a read-order one.** The gate: the baseline profile through the new loader reproduces §3.14b's landing exactly — **flagrants 0.148, points 118.3, FG% 46.9%, penalty rate 51.9%** (5-seed mean, seeds 1000–5000), **per-seed identical, not merely in aggregate**. **An era profile producing different numbers is the goal working, not a failure.** Both halves matter: without the first, profiles are useless; without the second, §3.16 cannot trust the baseline it recalibrates from. The 2026-08 cap rename is the worked example of the bar.

**The specific hazard #034's determinism note leaves, and why this pass is structurally safe from it:** §3.14b's severity roll is drawn **per foul**, its flagrant-2 sub-roll **only on a hit**, so draw count varies with how many fouls a game produced. **A mechanism that changes how many draws a possession takes breaks reproduction; one that changes the ORDER constants are read in is safe**, because constants are read *before* the rolls that consume them. **Every mechanism in A–F is of the second kind** — moving a value from an inlined literal to an instance accessor changes where a number comes from, never how many `rng.nextDouble()` calls happen. This is a design property, not a hope: **§3.15 introduces no branch, conditional or loop** (Scope), so no path exists by which draw count can move. A reproduction failure therefore means a *value* changed — a wrong default, a mis-parsed property, a units error — and surfaces as total failure rather than subtle drift, which is the good outcome.

**Decision H — the doc-drift generator is OUT (todo.md Q7, decided deliberately rather than by omission).** The generated constants-reference table stays a separate backlog chore. The profile shape does not make it nearly free — if anything F's dump serves the cheaper half of that need from the runtime side — and folding a doc-generation tool into a pass gated on byte-for-byte reproduction adds surface for no gate-relevant benefit. **It remains worth doing**, just not here.

**Decision I — calibration targets are declared BASELINE-ONLY; off-baseline the harness prints profile-vs-baseline DELTAS instead (user call).** todo.md's Q8, forced by the league-styles goal. calibration.md remains the source of truth for the targets, and **they belong to the baseline profile**. A baseline run prints them exactly as today — `(target ~112 — CONTESTED, see calibration.md)`, `(target ~26)`, `(target ~0.39)`. **On any other profile the target strings are suppressed**, and each stat instead prints its delta against the baseline landing: `Points / team / game: 101.4 (baseline 118.3, −16.9)`. **The instrument must not lie about the comparison it exists to enable** — an era profile is *supposed* to miss most targets, and a wall-of-misses report trains a reader to ignore the misses that matter.

**Two boundaries make this a decision rather than a formatting choice.** First, **§3.15 does not invent a second target set** — **a delta is not a target**: it claims nothing about what a number *should* be, only what changed, which is exactly the claim the comparison needs. Per-profile expected ranges would create a second unsourced table beside the one §3.16 exists to fix (#017's don't-fabricate-a-constraint rule, applied to targets). Second, **the baseline figures the deltas measure against are §3.14b's shipped landing, held as a named constant set** — not re-measured per run, which would make a delta depend on whichever baseline run someone did last.

**Rationale**: *(overall)* The pass turns on a compile-time rule rather than a taste question: `static final` primitives are inlined into callers, so every "keep the old call sites" shortcut reintroduces a silent-disagreement mode. *(A)* Comparing hypotheses is a deltas problem. *(B)* Static-mutable cannot express a per-simulation profile in a concurrent Spring app, the stated destination; paying the conversion once beats paying it twice. *(C)* With the threading happening anyway, a narrow allow-list buys nothing and leaves two classes of constant to explain. *(D)* The one failure this pass cannot tolerate is a run that isn't the config it claims. *(F)* Level 1 matches the manual seed-loop workflow already in use. *(G)* No branch, conditional or loop ⇒ draw count cannot move. *(I)* An instrument whose report reads as failure when it is working gets ignored.

**Trade-off**: *(A)* Values and their reasoning now live in **two files**, so the pointer comments must stay honest as the javadoc is condensed later; and `SimConfig` can no longer be constructed without its properties file, making that file a build-time dependency of the test suite. **D's completeness check is load-bearing, not a nicety.** *(B)* **323 call sites change, 239 in tests** — the largest mechanical diff in Phase 3, in a pass forbidden to change a number; mitigated by the gate being total rather than subtle (G), but real review burden. A mistyped property key becomes a runtime concern, paid by D. *(C)* 57 constants is a wide surface for D to validate and F to print, and the `double[]` needs a bespoke parse. *(F)* Comparing profiles stays a read-two-reports operation. *(I)* Off-baseline runs lose the at-a-glance target check.

**Alternatives considered**: *(A)* **Override-only profiles** (Java keeps the values, files carry deltas) — rejected: leaves the numbers in the code, the opposite of the stated intent. **Java defaults *plus* a baseline file, with a test asserting they agree** — rejected: two copies caught by a test is weaker than one copy where drift is impossible. **`application-<name>.properties`** — rejected: Spring's own profile namespace, already load-bearing for the datasource. *(B)* **`static` non-final fields** — rejected: fixes the inlining for almost no diff, but makes the profile process-global (cannot express a per-game profile in a concurrent app) and exposes public mutable state to order-dependent test pollution. **`static final` aliases so tests compile unchanged** — rejected: constant variables are inlined at each caller's compile time, so an alias is a second value with no live link to the first; `SimConfigTest`'s 80 assertions would look authoritative and be wrong off-baseline. **A parallel object the constants delegate to** — rejected: `static final` fields cannot delegate at all. *(C)* **A narrow ~20 era-knob allow-list** — rejected: its only merit was avoiding the `PlayerGameState` threading, which B does regardless. **Excluding the retention cap** — rejected: #034 B's fence protects the baseline, which a profile cannot touch. **Profiling the `*_SENSITIVITY` family** — deferred, not refused: one talent-spread knob spread across 15 constants (ideas.md). *(D)* **Document, don't enforce** — rejected: permits the silently-wrong run. *(F)* **An in-process comparison table** — deferred: the better instrument, but extra machinery in a reproduction-gated pass. *(I)* **Per-profile expected ranges** — rejected: era target sets are §3.16's research and would create a second unsourced table.

**Status of §3.15 decisions**: A–I all resolved, covering todo.md's eight open questions (Q1→A, Q2→B+E, Q3→C+D, Q4→A, Q5→F, Q6→B, Q7→H, Q8→I). **Net schema change: NONE. Net OpenAPI change: NONE. New possession branch: NONE — `possession-flow.puml` does not change.** New engine pieces: **57 constants converted from `public static final` to initializer-free instance state on the existing `SimConfig` bean**, with 26 (9 rules + 16 machinery + `PERSONAL_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME`) left static; **`@ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "sim")` + `@Validated`** on that bean (**no custom loader and no new flag** — Spring profile composition does the selecting); a test asserting baseline lists every profilable field; a `SimConfig` reference threaded into `PlayerGameState`, `GameData`, `MissedShotResolver` and `GametimeServiceImp`; **two profile files** (`application-baseline.properties` — all 57, grouped by what the knob does — and `application-nineties.properties`, deltas only); `spring.profiles.active` becoming `local,baseline` / `test,baseline`; an effective-config block + baseline-only targets on the harness. **Determinism note: no RNG draw is added, removed, or reordered** (G) — only the *provenance* of already-read values changes. The execute-ready task sequence is todo.md's §3.15 execution plan. **Open-at-execution**: accessor naming for the 57 (`config.baseThree()` assumed — the *key* format is settled kebab, A); the list-parsing convention for `FOUL_TROUBLE_SIT_PROBABILITY` (comma-separated vs. indexed keys) plus its length-7 validation; where the loader reads profile files from on the classpath (**not** the `application-*` namespace, A); whether Spring's `@ConfigurationProperties` binder is used for the binding; and **whether `PlayerGameState` takes a `SimConfig` field or is passed one per call** — **6** of its 17 reads are profilable (11 became static once `MAX_ENERGY` moved) and **none is in the constructor**, so the field is the assumption and the 9 construction sites are the thing to measure first.

**§3.15 follow-up (carry forward)**:
- **The in-process profile comparison table** (F, deferred) — harness-only work, made possible by B's instance fields. Natural time is §3.16, the largest multi-config sweep the project will run.
- **Per-game profiles in the running app are UNBLOCKED but NOT BUILT** (B). What remains is persistence of which profile a game or league ran, plus an API — schema, therefore **Phase 4+** (#014/#017). §3.15 builds the substrate and stops.
- **F's effective-config dump partially closes backlog.md's harness-self-verification chore** — its load-bearing half. The other assertions there (FT-source counts summing with no `UNKNOWN`, points reconciling with the event log) stay filed.
- **Two chores need re-scoping after this ships**: the generated constants-reference table (H) — `baseline.properties` now *is* most of one — and **trimming `SimConfig`'s ~640 javadoc lines**, folded into the Phase-4 condense pass (A). That pass should also sweep javadoc describing constants that are no longer `static final`.
- **TALENT SPREAD is parked as a future profile axis** ([ideas.md](ideas.md)): the 15 `*_SENSITIVITY` constants are one conceptual knob — how far apart good and bad players are. Promoting it wants a single multiplier over the family plus its own calibration pass, not 15 keys.
- **§3.16 inherits the profile as its instrument**, plus two obligations: notes name the profile beside the seed (F), and the CONTESTED points/FG% targets stay baseline-only (I). **§3.16 owns re-solving them; §3.15 moved no target and created no second target set.** `PERSONAL_FOULS_PER_TEAM_GAME`'s coupling to the emergent foul rate is likewise untouched and still §3.16's (#034 G) — a profile raising the foul rate makes that divisor wrong *for that profile*, the first thing a future era-tuning pass must confront.
- **The parked cap 3→5 idea still applies to the BASELINE value** (ideas.md, #034 B) and still needs its own recalibration pass; C making the cap profilable does not unpark it.
